Even when interruptions do not cause learners to disengage from the task at hand, they can increase negative affect and frustration, 16, 20, 21 plausibly interfering with effective learning.

Further, a large body of macroeconomic research documents the relationship between connectivity and broad economic and education outcomes. 22, 23 Though connectivity effects are generally positive, estimates vary widely, 8, 22 partly because this work relates aggregate connectivity to aggregate educational outcomes, which may be poor proxies for individual learners' experiences. The present research is unique in examining fine-grained data tracking how delays shape learners' experience in the moment.

In a preliminary test of microdelay effects, we conducted a stylized online experiment that randomly assigned participants to read popular-science excerpts with or without microdelays in page loading (N = 598; preregistered at https://aspredicted.org/jq36-8yw9.pdf on 17 February, 2024; see Supplementary Information Section S3). Microdelays markedly reduced the share of participants who read the full excerpt (70% without delays vs. 54% with delays, Bayes Factor (BF) = 559.06, representing "very strong" support for a difference between the two groups). 24 Below, we present our primary evidence: two longitudinal quasi-experimental field studies demonstrating that microdelays can impair learner engagement and achievement. First, in an online mathematics platform used by U.S. primary and secondary school students in 43 states, even brief loading delays were tied to: lower assignment completion rates; more sessions needed to complete assignments; higher rates of getting sidetracked while assignments loaded; and lower assignment scores. In a second study, among aspiring coders in a global online learning platform, microdelays predicted lower engagement (both in time spent and pages visited), higher distraction rates, fewer completed lessons, and a lower likelihood of reaching a key course milestone.

Of course, mere correlations between load times and educational outcomes could arise from confounding factors tied to both load times and learning. For example, learners with slower connections are more likely to come from disadvantaged backgrounds, 25 which can depress educational achievement through various pathways. 26 Similarly, longer lessons may both take more time to load and be more difficult (or easier) to complete. To address these concerns, we leveraged within-person, quasi-random fluctuations in load times to examine the causal effect of microdelays on learning. In addition, both studies employed multiverse analyses to ensure the robustness of our findings across a broad range of model specifications. 27 By triangulating evidence across populations, contexts, and methods, our research is unique in demonstrating that seemingly trivial microdelays can disrupt learners, contributing to a persistent but subtle digital divide.

Results

Microdelays Disrupt Math Learning Among Primary and Secondary School Students

This study (preregistered at https://aspredicted.org/fgfg-qjq8.pdf on 1 November, 2024 and https://aspredicted.org/yn4zf8.pdf on 7 January, 2026) examined the link between microdelays and learning using data from ASSISTments.org, an online mathematics education platform used in primary and secondary schools across 43 U.S. states. Teachers use ASSISTments to administer mathematics assignments to students. How students perform on ASSISTments is consequential because it both: (a) forms part of their school grade and (b) robustly predicts high-stakes standardized test scores as well as college enrollment. [28] [29] [30] Investigating microdelays in formal education is critical because disruptions in this context could have lasting consequences for students' academic trajectories and, therefore, their educational and career opportunities.

Our dataset included all ASSISTments.org data gathered between September 18 and December 18, 2024. Each observation represented a single assignment attempted by a single student. The data comprised 228,734 such observations from 26,707 students belonging to 1,553 classes and 373 schools (see Supplementary Table S1 for descriptive statistics). Load times in the data were generally brief, with 94% lasting less than 3 seconds. This likely reflects both: (a) concerted efforts by ASSISTments engineers to minimize latency and (b) selection effects whereby schools that subscribe to this online service are more likely to have fast and reliable internet connections. This limited range meant that our dataset primarily included microdelays (0-3 seconds), our key focus.

We tested whether longer load times were tied to reduced student engagement and achievement across four outcomes: (a) completion (whether or not a student completed an assignment), (b) sessions-to-complete (the number of sessions a student needed in order to complete an assignment), (c) distraction (whether a student failed to start an assignment shortly after it loaded), and (d) assignment scores (the percentage of correct answers on an assignment; an additional outcome is reported separately due to technical artifacts in measurement, see Supplementary Section S10).

To isolate the causal role of microdelays, we examined within-person fluctuations in load times. We did this by person-mean-centering load times, subtracting a student's average load time from each observed load time. 31 Because these momentary changes are by definition orthogonal to any stable person-level characteristic (e.g., socioeconomic status), this approach controls for all time-invariant (between-person) confounds, in addition to observed potential time-varying confounds such as time of day. 32, 33 Figure 1 illustrates the link between load times and learning outcomes using multilevel Generalized Additive Models (GAMs), a method that allows flexible modeling and visualization of non-linear relationships in clustered data (see also Supplementary Figure S1 for unadjusted descriptive statistics). As shown, longer load times were tied to impaired learning, even at the shortest delays (e.g., 1-2 seconds). Because the combination of splines that make up GAM coefficients is difficult to interpret, we proceeded to systematically test the links between microdelays and learning using linear multilevel models. All models included random effects for students nested within classes, as well as cross-classified random effects for assignment ID, to account for assignment-level heterogeneity (e.g., difficulty and topic). We also included fixed effects for: (1) the number of problems in an assignment (because longer assignments both take longer to load and may correlate with higher scores or longer completion times), (2) temporal factors (time of day, day of week, and an indicator for school hours), and (3) school-level demographics (a school's share of students receiving free or reduced-price lunch and the share of non-Hispanic White students). Table 1 reports regression results for each main outcome. These analyses reveal consistent harmful impacts of microdelays on learning: A 1-second increase in load time was associated with: (a) a 1.1 percentage-point decrease in assignment completion; (b) 0.07 additional sessions required to complete an assignment; (c) a 2.8 percentage-point increase in likelihood of distraction; and (d) a 0.7-point decrease in assignment scores (all ps < .001; slope significance assessed using two-sided t-tests with Satterthwaite's approximation for degrees of freedom). Note that although the per-assignment effects of a 1-second delay here are modest, their magnitude is comparable to that of typical effects in field-based, well-powered education interventions. [34] [35] [36] Crucially, unlike one-off interventions, microdelays recur over the course of days, weeks, and months, and thus exert a cumulative drag on learning that exceeds each microdelay's immediate effect.

We used a multiverse approach 27 to assess the robustness of these results across 150 models varied along several dimensions (see Supplementary Table S2): (a) outcome, (b) outlierhandling method, (c) inclusion of time-varying-and/or school-level covariates, and (d) inclusion of an indicator for whether a student began the assignment during school hours or not (to distinguish in-class work from at-home work). 37 Because load times included extreme outliers even after preregistered exclusions-as might be expected in messy field data-we foreground analyses that account for outliers (nonetheless, similar results emerge even when including models that do not account for outliers; see Supplementary Sections S4-S5 and S10, and Supplementary Figures S3-S7 for all preregistered analysis results).

Multiverse results underscored the robustness of these findings across all outcomes: In total, 132 of 150 (88%) models demonstrated a statistically significant harmful effect of load times, 17 models (11%) demonstrated a non-significant effect, and 1 model (1%) demonstrated a significant beneficial effect.

Microdelays Disrupt Learning in a Global Online Education Platform

Having demonstrated that microdelays disrupt learning among U.S. elementary and secondary school students, our second field study (preregistered at https://aspredicted.org/mc7is5.pdf on 16 February, 2026) extends these findings using data from freeCodeCamp, a popular online professional development platform. In freeCodeCamp courses, users learn how to code by completing hands-on projects broken down into small steps (lessons). Our analyses focused on its Spanish-language website, which serves a large user base of learners from Latin America. Extending our findings to this global sample is meaningful because many freeCodeCamp learners reside in middle-income countries where bandwidth access is uneven. 38, 39 Because most freeCodeCamp users report using the platform to pursue a technology-sector career, 40 investigating the impact of brief lags in this context could highlight an overlooked barrier to global economic mobility, especially in light of studies underscoring the macro-level economic benefits of access to high-speed internet. 23 Our primary, preregistered dataset included all visits to the Spanish-speaking version of freeCodeCamp over a two-month period (July 4-Sep 4, 2023; we also analyze an extended fourmonth span of data on an exploratory basis, see Supplementary Section S9 and Supplementary Tables S11-S12). Each observation in the dataset represents a single visit by a given user. The data included 186,230 visits associated with 55,543 unique user IDs (see descriptive statistics in Supplementary Table S7).

We tested whether load times predicted learner engagement and achievement across a range of outcomes, including: (a) engagement (time spent with the platform window in focus), (b) number of pages visited, (c) focus (during a given visit, the share of time spent with the platform in focus, as opposed to other tabs), and (d) lessons completed. In line with common practice for measuring web engagement, measures (a) and (b) were additionally dichotomized to test the likelihood of a user engaging with the website for more than a given threshold (e.g., one minute, with additional thresholds tested below) or visiting more than one page (equivalent to bounce rate, a commonly-used statistic for tracking online engagement). 41 Figure 2 illustrates the link between load times and learning outcomes (using multilevel GAMs; see also Supplementary Figure S8 for unadjusted descriptive statistics). Longer load times were tied to impaired engagement and achievement across outcomes, even at the shortest delays (e.g., 1-2 seconds), highlighting that even minuscule delays can hinder learning. We again tested these links using linear multilevel models. All models included random effects for users nested within countries, as well as crossed random effects for each visit's starting page URL to account for page-level characteristics such as size and content. We also included fixed effects for temporal factors (time of day and day of week).

As before, we used multiverse analyses to assess the results' robustness across 180 models across several dimensions (see Supplementary Table S8 and Supplementary Figures S9-15), including (a) outcome, (b) outlier-handling transformation method, and (c) inclusion of covariates. We highlight regression results for each main outcome in Table 2. As shown, a 1second increase in load time was associated with: a 4.6 percentage-point decrease in the likelihood of engagement; a 5.2 percentage-point decrease in the likelihood of visiting more than one page; a 1.5 percentage-point decrease in focus; and completing 0.20 fewer lessons per visit (all ps < .001; slope significance assessed with two-sided t-tests using Satterthwaite's approximation for degrees of freedom).

Multiverse results highlighted the robustness of these findings: 180 out of 180 (100%) models demonstrated a statistically significant and harmful effect of load times, with no models (0%) showing either non-significant or positive effects. The effects observed in this dataset are of clear practical importance: a 4-5 percentage-point reduction in engagement per second implies that a learner facing a 2-second delay is roughly 10 percentage points less likely to meaningfully engage with the platform on a given visit. These effects are considerably larger than those found in the math student data. This may reflect the self-directed setting in which freeCodeCamp users typically operate; this is in contrast with students, who typically complete assignments in class, supervised by a teacher, and on school laptops that offer few distractions-constraints that could partially buffer against delays' disruptive impact. Using Simonsohn et al.'s approach for inference in multiverse analyses with 500 bootstrap samples, 42 we find that the observed share of significant harmful effects found in our multiverse (100%) exceeds the highest value in our bootstrap simulations (48%), i.e., p < .002.

In additional analyses assessing the impact of microdelays on cumulative achievement, we tested whether users with slower connections were less likely to achieve a key milestone in freeCodeCamp's flagship course-completing the first project, which involves building a simple website. We used a Cox proportional hazards model to predict project completion from each user's log-transformed average load time. We found that users who experienced longer delays were less likely to complete the first project, HR [95% CI] = 0.90 [0.86, 0.95], p < .001, an effect that persisted across a range of outlier-handling methods (see Supplementary Table S9; note that in contrast with our other within-person analyses this is a between-person analysis and therefore does not inherently eliminate confounding by individual characteristics).

Discussion

In two large-scale longitudinal field studies, loading delays as brief as 1-2 seconds predicted impaired learning among both U.S. math students and a global sample of aspiring coders. When pages took longer to load, learner engagement slipped, distraction rose, and achievement fell, a pattern that held across hundreds of model specifications in multiverse analyses. As online education proliferates across K-12 classrooms, universities, and workplaces, the aggregate toll of microdelays may continue to grow.

Our investigation provides unique converging evidence that microdelays causally impair online learning. First, our analyses leveraged quasi-random within-person variation in load times, an approach that eliminates confounding from stable individual differences (e.g. socioeconomic status, regional infrastructure), 32, 33 as well as observed time-varying confounds (e.g., time of day and day of week). Second, we augmented field results with a randomized controlled experiment in which randomly imposed delays markedly depressed educational task completion. Taken together, these data strongly suggest that microdelays causally impair learning.

How do brief lags on the order of just a few seconds disrupt learning? One possibility is that microdelays interrupt learners, leading them to disengage from the task at hand. Prior smallscale ethnographic research on knowledge workers observed that after experiencing brief interruptions, workers often switch tasks, which in turn can harm task completion. 17 This account aligns with our findings that microdelays were tied to increased distraction in the math student data, and increased time spent on other tabs in the aspiring coder data. Notably, we found that microdelays harmed aspiring coders-who were typically self-directed-more strongly than math students, who mostly worked in class, under teacher supervision, and on school laptops where distractions were absent or inconvenient. In other words, in self-directed environments that impose little cost on task-switching, a moment's wait could be enough to send attention elsewhere-though we do not test this mechanism directly here.

Microdelays exerted sizable effects among self-directed coders (𝛽s of 0.05 to 0.11). Yet even the more modest effects we found among math students (𝛽s of 0.01 to 0.053) are comparable in magnitude to the median intervention commissioned by the U.S. Department of Education (0.03 SD) and the marginal effect of raising per-pupil spending by $1,000 (0.005 to 0.009 SD). 34, 36, 43 Furthermore, in both of our datasets, microdelay effects persisted over the course of months. Such repeated exposure could exert an outsize cumulative impact even in cases where the individual effect of each episode is small. 44 Limitations of the present investigation are worth highlighting. First, neither of our datasets includes individual-level data on socioeconomic status (SES). This omission does not impact the validity of our key claims, since our within-person approach removes confounding from stable individual differences like SES. However, it does preclude quantifying the extent to which microdelays' harms are distributed across rich and poor learners. Even so, given the welldocumented link between lower income and slower internet, the cost of microdelays likely falls disproportionately on disadvantaged learners. 25 Second, our outcomes are mostly proximal markers of learning, rather than end-of-year grades. Prior work has shown that students' performance on ASSISTments robustly predicts standardized test scores and even college enrollment, [28] [29] [30] suggesting that the harms we document could persist long-term. Further, because students' ASSISTments scores are typically part of their end-of-year grade, the effects we show influence end-of-year grades by construction, though the precise impact will depend on how heavily ASSISTments scores are weighted in final grades (a weight that varies across teachers and that we do not observe). Finally, our results do not speak to whether faster internet connections would improve learning outcomes for all learners and in all contexts. 8 For instance, in some cases a faster internet connection might facilitate accessing online temptations like social media.

Our findings carry implications for policymakers, particularly in light of debates over whether high-speed internet should be considered a basic utility rather than a luxury. 45, 46 Because slower connections disproportionately affect lower-income households, 25 microdelays are most likely to affect already-disadvantaged learners, thereby compounding existing educational inequalities. Our findings may thus underscore the case for treating fast internet as essential infrastructure, and paying attention to seemingly minor connectivity disparities. Yet support for such policies may be marred by laypeople's underestimation of microdelays' impact-echoing prior findings that people underweight friction when explaining behavior. 47, 48 Likewise, microdelays may constitute an overlooked policy tool for curbing engagement with apps that compete with educational activities. The United Kingdom and Australia recently announced social media platform bans for children under 16, but the enforcement of such bans is not straightforward. 49 Beyond policy, our findings carry implications for education technology providers. Our findings suggest that online learning platforms should be wary of bandwidth-heavy features that could enrich the experience of well-connected learners while penalizing those on slower networks. Platforms could also dynamically adapt users' experience based on their connection speed, much as streaming services automatically downshift video resolution to avoid loading delays.

Microdelays are easy to dismiss as a minor nuisance. Yet, as our findings show, even fleeting delays can shape whether learners engage, persist, and succeed. As learning increasingly moves onto screens, microdelays risk widening the digital divide by undermining learners with slower connections.

Methods

Ethics Statement

This research complies with all relevant ethical regulations. The randomized controlled experiment and the preliminary survey were approved by the University of Pennsylvania Institutional Review Board (randomized controlled experiment protocol number: #853509; preliminary survey protocol number: #823240), and all participants provided informed consent. The two field studies used de-identified data collected by ASSISTments.org and freeCodeCamp as part of their ongoing operations and therefore do not constitute human subjects research. Data were shared with the research team in de-identified form and stored securely. The use of these data for research is consistent with the platforms' terms of service and privacy policies. In the publicly deposited ASSISTments dataset, noise was added to school-level demographic variables so that individual schools cannot be identified; all analyses reported here used the original variables.

Software

All analyses in this paper were performed in R 4.4.1, with the exception of some supplementary tasks (data preprocessing for the aspiring coder dataset and coding open-text responses for the preliminary survey), which used Python 3.11.2. This paper's repositories include environment files that specify versions for all software and packages required to reproduce this paper's results.

Study 1 (Math Students)

Study data were logged via the ASSISTments web platform. The dataset included all assignments that were started between Sep 18 and Dec 18, 2024. Data were exported on January 17, 2026. Demographic data for each school were taken from the National Center for Education Statistics' Common Core of Data (NCES CCD). 50 Note that, at ASSISTments' request, to avoid identifying specific schools in the dataset, the publicly available data files add noise to schoollevel demographic variables. All analyses in this paper use the original demographic variables.

Definition of Key Variables

Load time. The elapsed latency (in milliseconds) until an assignment is fully loaded. The ASSISTments web application is designed so that when a student starts an assignment, all problems in that assignment are loaded in one batch, such that the first problem appears only after all problems are fully loaded. Consequently, there should be virtually no delays between problems within the same assignment. In cases where there were multiple load times for an assignment (e.g., if a student refreshed the page), load times were averaged. Our main analyses use load times winsorized at the 95 th percentile (3.2 seconds), the threshold in our preregistered grid closest to our 3-second operationalization of microdelays. Results using all other thresholds are reported as part of the multiverse analyses.

Sessions.

A session starts whenever a student goes on the ASSISTments web application for the first time or once at least 30 minutes have elapsed since the previous time they went on ASSISTments. A session ends once a student is inactive on ASSISTments for over 30 minutes, with session end time set retroactively so that the 30 final inactive minutes are not included in calculations. For example, if a user goes on the website at 8am, is active until 8:10am, is inactive between 8:10-8:20, then returns at 8:20-8:25, this would count as a single session, because the period of inactivity (8:10-8:20) lasted less than 30 minutes.

Completion.

Whether an assignment was completed (meaning the student submitted an answer to all problems) by the time the data were exported.

Sessions to complete.

The number of unique sessions it took students to complete an assignment. Naturally, this measure is only valid for completed assignments, so it was only calculated for them.

Distraction.

The lag between the time an assignment finished loading and the time a student started the assignment. In principle, a student should start an assignment right after loading is complete, leading to a short lag, e.g. up to a few seconds. A longer lag suggests that the student switched tasks. In our preregistered multiverse analyses, we test two thresholds for what constitutes a long lag: 10 seconds and 1 minute. We additionally test additional thresholds, up to 5 minutes, on an exploratory basis (see Supplementary Table S6).

Score. Assignment scores range from 0-100%, generally representing the share of problems that a student answered correctly in a given assignment. Students can use up to 3 hints per problem, with the 3rd hint simply providing them with the answer. Each hint used in an ASSISTments problem reduces the score for that specific problem by ⅓. For instance, if a student used 2 hints, their maximum score for that specific problem would be 33%; if a student used 3 hints such that they simply received the answer, they would receive 0% for that problem. ASSISTments assignments can include both closed-ended and open-ended problems. Openended problems require a teacher to manually score the problem. In the case that a teacher did not score a student's answer to a given open-ended problem, the calculation of that assignment's total score excluded the ungraded problem(s).

Study 2 (Aspiring Coders)

Data

The initial dataset included all visits to freeCodeCamp's Spanish-language website between July 4 and Sep 4, 2023. As might be expected for a Spanish-speaking website, most (83%) users hailed from Latin America. We only included visits from desktop or laptop computers, because freeCodeCamp's coding courses-which require extensive typing-are functionally inaccessible on mobile devices.

Some visits did not have a load time recorded (81,267, or 30.38%). This can occur due to technical problems or due to a learner returning to an open freeCodeCamp tab that they opened previously (more than 30 minutes earlier): because the page is already loaded, a page load event will not be triggered in Google Analytics. Observations with missing load times were excluded, per our preregistration.

Definition of Key Variables

All study data were recorded via Google Analytics, which automatically logs a range of variables during user interactions with the site. We detail below how each variable is defined (readers interested in further information may refer to Google's documentation: https://developers.google.com/analytics/devguides/collection/ga4).

Load time.

The elapsed latency (in milliseconds) until the first page in a given visit fully loads. Because we only observed load times for the first page, first-page load times serve as a proxy for load times in subsequent pages. In visits associated with more than one load time (likely because a user refreshed the page), load times were averaged. Because load times in this dataset were far more dispersed than in the math student dataset, our main analyses use load times winsorized at the 80th percentile (5.8 seconds), the threshold in our preregistered grid closest to our 3-second operationalization of microdelays.

Visits. This is calculated in largely the same way as sessions were calculated in the math student dataset. Specifically, a visit starts whenever a user visits the website for the first time or after at least 30 minutes elapsed since their previous visit. A visit ends once a user is inactive on freeCodeCamp for over 30 minutes, with visit end time set retroactively so that the 30 final inactive minutes are not included in calculations.

Active time. The total sum of engaged time in a given visit (in seconds), meaning time during which the freeCodeCamp browser tab was in focus on a learner's computer.

Engagement. Binary engagement measures were calculated by assessing whether active time exceeds a certain threshold (e.g., 1 minute). Binary engagement metrics were used in some models because often, the marginal value of increasing engagement is not linear: for instance, increasing the time a learner spends from 1 minute to 5 minutes is more valuable than increasing it from 181 to 185 minutes. Such threshold metrics are commonly used by firms to measure website and app engagement (e.g., "bounce rate"). 41 Nonetheless, we show that the result holds both when using continuous measures as well as binary metrics with different thresholds.

Total visit length.

The total duration of a visit (in seconds) from start to finish, excluding the final 30 minutes of inactivity. This is calculated via the time interval between the firstrecorded Google Analytics event and the last-recorded event.

Focus. This measure represents active time divided by total visit length. The result is a score ranging between 0-100%, where higher percentages represent higher engagement. In illustration, if a user was engaged for 5 minutes of a 10-minute visit, their focus score would be 50%. Because focus is not a meaningful metric for very short visits, we only calculated focus for visits longer than 1 minute. Nonetheless, similar results emerge when including visits shorter than 1 minute.

Pages visited.

The number of pages a user saw in a given visit. Similarly to engagement, in additional models we used a dichotomized measure assessing whether a user visited one page or more than one page during a given visit (equivalent to bounce rate).

Time of day.

The time of day (in hours, 0-23) when a visit started. Time of day was assigned based on the country a visit originated from. For countries that spanned multiple time zones (e.g., Brazil), the most common time zone was used.

User ID.

An ID that uniquely identifies a given device across visits.

Country.

The country where the visit originated. To assist with model convergence, countries where few (fewer than 200) visits originated during the study period were assigned a value of "other."

Starting Page Identifier.

The URL of the first page in a given visit. To assist with model convergence, pages that received few (fewer than 200) visits during the study period were assigned a value of "other".

Randomized Controlled Experiment

Prescreen and Sample

To participate in the experiment, participants had to qualify based on a prescreen survey. In the prescreen, they saw the title and picture of 9 popular science blog posts and rated each on a scale from 1 (not at all interested) to 7 (extremely interested). Participants received $0.20 for completing the prescreen. Out of 1,500 prescreen participants, 970 (64.67%) qualified for the main study, 612 (40.80%) opted to participate in the main study (i.e., indicated that they wanted to read the full excerpt), and 607 (40.47%) began the main study. Of the 607 who began the main study, 9 participants (1.48%) had duplicate entries and so were excluded on an exploratory basis, yielding a final sample of 598 participants (310 women, 272 men, 13 genderqueer/other, 3 trans men; M age = 44.56, SD age = 14.00).

Design and Sample Size Planning

This study used a between-person design with participants randomly assigned to one of two conditions (delay: 3-second vs. no delay), and sample size determined using a sequential Bayesian design. 51 Our preregistered plan was to recruit participants in batches of 500 each, with data collection to continue until results sufficiently supported either the null hypothesis (no difference between conditions), or the alternative hypothesis (some difference between conditions, regardless of the direction of that difference). Specifically, our preregistered threshold for stopping data collection was a Bayes factor of 10 or 1/10.

Note that our preregistered sample sizes refer to the number of participants recruited to complete the prescreen. The actual final sample size is not knowable in advance since it depends on the share of participants who qualify, as well as the share of qualifying participants who agree to participate in the study.

Manipulation

The experiment was programmed in Qualtrics. In the delay condition, participants saw a simulated loading animation before each page. This animation, designed to resemble a typical loading screen, displayed for 3 seconds before automatically advancing to the next page.
